### What the booklet teaches, lesson by lesson

#### Lesson 1 — First-order linear ODEs (pp. 9–18 theory)

- **Definitions (p. 9):** an ODE is a relation between a function,
  its first derivative, the variable and constants; a solution is a
  function satisfying the equation; the order is the highest
  derivative present. Example 1: y = ke^(−3x) is the general
  solution of y′ + 3y = 0.
- **Verifying solutions (p. 10):** Example 2 checks y = ke^(−x/2)
  solves 2y′ + y = 0, and y = 5 tan 5x solves y′ − y² = 25
  (both correct).
- **Direct integration (pp. 10–11):** dy/dx = f(x) ⇒
  y = ∫f(x)dx + C; particular solution from y(x₀) = y₀.
  Example 3: y′ = 2x/(x²+1) → y = ln(x²+1) + C;
  y′ = 6x² + 4x + 5 → y = 2x³ + 2x² + 5x + C;
  y′ = xe^(x²) → y = (1/2)e^(x²) + C (all correct).
- **Separable equations (pp. 12–13):** g(y)dy = f(x)dx form.
  Example 4 classifies four equations (separable: dy/dx =
  2x(y+1)/y and dy/dx = e^(x+y); not directly separable:
  dy/dx = (y²+x²)/(xy) — homogeneous — and dy/dx = ln(xy));
  solves the separable ones with y(2) = 0:
  y − ln|y+1| = x² − 4 and y = −ln(1 + e² − eˣ) (both correct).
  Example 5: y′ = 3 + y and y′ = y² − 4 are separable;
  y′ = sin(x+y) and y′ = (y+1+xy²)/(xy) are not; with y(0) = −2
  the solutions are y = eˣ − 3 and the constant solution y = −2
  (correct — the equilibrium solution is spotted, not missed).
- **Homogeneous y′ + ay = 0 (pp. 14–15):** general solution
  y = Ae^(−ax), derived by separation. Examples 6–8 with initial
  conditions and graphs (y′ + y = 0 with y(0) = ±1 and y(1) = 1;
  −y′ + 2y = 0 with y(3) = −2 → y = −2e^(2(x−3));
  3y′ + 6y = 0 through (−4, 2) → y = 2e^(−2(x+4)) — all correct).
- **Non-homogeneous y′ + ay = p(x) (pp. 16–18):** solution =
  complementary + particular (undetermined coefficients, with a
  trial-form table by the shape of p(x): polynomial, exponential,
  trigonometric, including the resonance shift); and variation of
  the constant (A(x) method), derived in full. Example 9 solves
  y′ + y = 2eˣ both ways → y = Ce^(−x) + eˣ (correct).
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 19–26; practice exercises,
  pp. 27–47. P. 48 is blank.

#### Lesson 2 — Second-order linear ODEs (pp. 49–56 theory)

- **Homogeneous ay″ + by′ + cy = 0 (pp. 49–50):** superposition
  principle (with the Wronskian condition); characteristic equation
  ar² + br + c = 0; discriminant cases: Δ > 0 → y = Ae^(αx) +
  Be^(βx); Δ = 0 → y = (Ax + B)e^(αx); Δ < 0 → y = e^(αx)(A cos βx
  + B sin βx), derived via Euler's formula; special case
  y″ + ω²y = 0 → y = A cos ωx + B sin ωx.
- **Worked examples (pp. 51–52):** Δ > 0: y″ − 5y′ + 6y = 0 →
  Ae^(2x) + Be^(3x); y″ − y′ − 2y = 0 with y(0) = 4, y′(0) = 5 →
  e^(−x) + 3e^(2x). Δ = 0: y″ − 6y′ + 9y = 0 → (Ax+B)e^(3x);
  4y″ + 4y′ + y = 0 with y(0) = 2, y′(0) = −3 → 2(1−x)e^(−x/2).
  Δ < 0: y″ + 9y = 0 → A cos 3x + B sin 3x; y″ − 2y′ + 5y = 0
  with y(0) = 1, y′(0) = 3 → eˣ(cos 2x + sin 2x). All correct.
- **Non-homogeneous ay″ + by′ + cy = p(x) (pp. 53–56):**
  y = yc + yp; undetermined-coefficients choice table for p(x)
  (polynomial / ke^(λx) / M cos ωx + N sin ωx, with the x^s
  resonance shift). Examples: y″ − 3y′ + 2y = 2x² − 4x + 1 →
  C₁eˣ + C₂e^(2x) + x² + x + 1; y″ − 4y = 3eˣ → −eˣ particular;
  resonance y″ − 4y = 8e^(2x) → yp = 2xe^(2x); y″ + 4y = 6 cos x
  with y(0) = 3, y′(0) = 0 → y = cos 2x + 2 cos x. All correct.
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 57–65; practice exercises,
  pp. 66–89; chapter exercises, pp. 90–107. P. 108 is blank.

#### Lesson 3 — Applications (pp. 109–116 theory)

- **Models (pp. 109–112):** population growth dP/dt = kP,
  P = P₀e^(kt), doubling time ln 2/k; radioactive decay
  dN/dt = −λN, half-life T½ = ln 2/λ; Newton's law of cooling
  dT/dt = −k(T − Tm), T(t) = Tm + (T₀ − Tm)e^(−kt); falling body
  with air resistance mv′ = mg − kv, terminal velocity mg/k;
  spring–mass my″ + cy′ + ky = 0 (undamped, underdamped cases).
- **Worked models (pp. 112–116):** bacteria 1000 → 4000 in 3 h,
  P(5) ≈ 10,080 cells, reaches 16,000 in 6 h; coffee cooling
  95 °C → 70 °C in 5 min in a 20 °C room, T(15) ≈ 42.22 °C,
  reaches 40 °C in ≈ 16.3 min; Polonium-210 (half-life 140 days),
  12 mg → ≈ 8.49 mg after 70 days, 90% decayed in ≈ 465 days;
  falling 5 kg mass (k = 10 kg/s, g = 9.8), v(t) = 4.9(1 − e^(−2t)),
  terminal velocity 4.9 m/s, v(2) ≈ 4.81 m/s; RL circuit
  (R = 12 Ω, L = 3 H, E = 60 V), i(t) = 5(1 − e^(−4t)) A,
  steady state 5 A, i(0.5) ≈ 4.32 A. All correct.
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 117–125; practice exercises
  (including Carbon-14 dating), pp. 126–136.

#### Baccalaureate question bank (pp. 137–154)

- Part 1 (pp. 137–149): official Baccalaureate questions
  2014–2026 on differential equations, each tagged by year.
  Part 2 (pp. 150–154): selected extra Bac-preparation questions.
  (Counted from the contents list: two parts; the individual
  questions were not re-counted item by item.)

#### Solutions (pp. 155–186) and appendix (pp. 187–192)

- MCQ answer-key tables for Lessons 1–3 (100 answers each, on
  pp. 155, 162 and 174) and detailed solutions of the
  practice/chapter exercises (L1 pp. 156–161, L2 pp. 163–173,
  L3 pp. 175–176) and of the Bac bank (pp. 177–186).
  Spot-checked solutions read correctly.
- Appendix: first-order and second-order method summary tables,
  primitive/derivative formula table, glossary of key terms and
  model examples. References p. 192.

### Confirmed book errors and slips

- **TOC page-number error (p. 7):** the contents list gives the
  references as p. 193, but the book has only 192 pages and the
  references are printed on p. 192. Off by one.
- **Undetermined-coefficients table (p. 53) is incomplete for
  polynomials:** it gives the shift s = 0 if c ≠ 0 and s = 1 if
  c = 0 and b ≠ 0, but gives no rule for c = 0 and b = 0
  (e.g. y″ = Pₙ(x), where the correct shift is s = 2). The stated
  cases are right; the c = b = 0 case is missing.
- No worked-example or theorem error was found in the theory
  sections read (Lessons 1–3, checked item by item above). The MCQ
  answer keys were spot-checked only (Lesson 1 Q1–Q2 read correct).

### Honest negatives — what this booklet does NOT teach

- No systems of differential equations; no Laplace transforms;
  no power-series / Frobenius solutions; no numerical methods
  (Euler's method etc.).
- No second-order equations with non-constant coefficients
  (no Cauchy–Euler equations); no Bernoulli or Riccati equations;
  no exact equations or integrating factors beyond the linear
  first-order case (variation of the constant).
- No partial differential equations. The applications stay at
  first/second-order linear constant-coefficient models.
- The characteristic-equation method is taught only for constant
  coefficients.

### Held-copy defects

- Blank pages (zero text layer; same divider pattern as the
  Complex Numbers booklet, whose blanks were visually verified):
  PDF pp. 8, 48, 108, 136, 186 — at lesson/section boundaries,
  intentional divider blanks; no content is missing.
- No duplicated pages, no truncated scan, no unreadable pages.
  The book ends at printed p. 192 (references); no back-cover page.

### Page mapping

Printed page number = PDF page number throughout (offset 0).


## 4. `g12-limits.pdf` — Grade 12 Limits and Continuity booklet (Khem Putthi)

**File:** `g12-limits.pdf` — 300 PDF pages, 2,932,086 bytes,
SHA-256 `fefee3ebd9f5…` (full hash in `grab-results.json`).
**Read:** text-layer read (Khmer extracts legibly; formulae
fragment across lines) plus visual read of the cover, p. 2

verification and visually-checked pages). Page numbers below are
PRINTED page numbers; printed number = PDF page number

on printed p. 9 = PDF p. 9; footer "130" on PDF p. 130).

### Identity — NOT a MoEYS publication (correction to the brief)

### Contents (from the printed table of contents, pp. 6–7)

- Lesson 1: Summary lesson — p. 9
  - 1 Limits of functions — p. 9: 1.1 intuitive meaning of a
    limit; 1.2–1.10 nine formal definitions (ε–δ and M–N);
    1.11 limit laws; 1.12 theorems; 1.13 one-sided limits;
    1.14 infinite limits of irrational functions; 1.15 infinite
    limits of functions; 1.16 limits of trigonometric
    functions; 1.17 limits of exponential functions; 1.18
    limits of logarithmic functions.
  - 2 Continuity of functions — p. 20: 2.1 continuity at a
    point; 2.2 properties of continuous functions; 2.3
    continuity of composite functions; 2.4 continuity on an
    interval; 2.5 intermediate-value theorem.
- Lesson 2: Computation methods — p. 23
  - 1 Computing limits by definition (cases 1–9) — pp. 23–31;
    2 factorisation — p. 33 (0/0 case); 3 irrational-function
    limits — p. 34 (§§3.1–3.7: √A−B, A−√B, √A−√B, ∛A−B, A−∛B, ∛A−∛B, √A+√B forms); 4 limits at infinity
    (rational functions) — p. 45; 5 limits at infinity for
    irrational functions (+∞−∞ form) — p. 47; 6 trigonometric
    limits — p. 49 (§6.1 x→x₀, §6.2 x→∞); 7 exponential
    limits — p. 53; 8 logarithmic limits — p. 55; 9 the
    indeterminate forms 1^∞, ∞⁰, ∞^∞ — p. 57; 10 the form
    0×∞ — p. 59; 11 limits at infinity for real sequences —
    p. 60; 12 L'Hôpital's rule — p. 62.
- Lesson 3: Exercises and solutions (23 worked exercises) —
  p. 65.
- Lesson 4: Practice exercises for application (39 exercise
  sets) — p. 119.
- Lesson 5: Solutions to the practice exercises — p. 187.
- References — p. 299. P. 300 is blank.

### What the booklet teaches, lesson by lesson

#### Lesson 1 — Summary lesson (pp. 9–22 theory)

The most formal booklet in the series: the limit is built
from nine separate formal definitions, each with a worked
example and a "by definition, prove that…" exercise.

- **Definitions 1–9 (pp. 9–17):** 1.1 intuitive limit
  (f(x)→L as x→a); 1.2 ε–δ definition of lim f(x) = L
  (proved for lim 4x = 8 as x→2, α = ε/4; lim (3x+2) = 5
  as x→1, α = ε/3); 1.3 lim f(x) = +∞ as x→a (M–α);
  1.4 lim f(x) = −∞ as x→a; 1.5 lim f(x) = L as x→+∞
  (ε–N); 1.6 lim f(x) = L as x→−∞; 1.7 lim f(x) = +∞ as
  x→+∞; 1.8 lim f(x) = −∞ as x→+∞; 1.9 lim f(x) = −∞ as
  x→−∞; 1.10 lim f(x) = +∞ as x→−∞. Each with an example
  and a model exercise (e.g. lim 1/x² = +∞ as x→0 with
  α = 1/√M; lim (x+2) = +∞ as x→+∞ with N = |M−2|+1).
- **Limit laws (1.11, p. 17):** constant, scalar multiple,
  sum, difference, product, quotient (denominator limit ≠ 0),
  power.
- **Theorems (1.12, pp. 17–18):** polynomial and rational
  limits by substitution; nth-root limits; composition
  (stated loosely — see minor points); trigonometric limits
  by substitution (sin, cos, tan, cot).
- **One-sided limits (1.13, p. 18):** left/right limits; a
  two-sided limit exists iff the one-sided limits agree.
- **Formula tables (1.14–1.18, pp. 18–20):** infinite-limit
  formulas (lim ax² = ±∞ by the sign of a; lim a/x = 0;
  lim x/a = ∞ — see Errors); standard trig limits
  (sin x/x → 1, sin ax/ax → 1, (1−cos ax)/x² → a²/2,
  sin²ax/x² → a², (1−cos x)/x → 0); exponential limits
  ((eˣ−1)/x → 1, (aˣ−1)/x → ln a, (1+x)^(1/x) → e,
  eˣ/xⁿ → +∞, xⁿ/eˣ → 0, aˣ → +∞ or 0 by a); logarithmic
  limits (ln(1+x)/x → 1, ln x → −∞ as x→0⁺, ln x → +∞ as
  x→+∞, (ln x)/x → 0, (ln x)/(x−1) → 1 as x→1, x ln x → 0,
  xⁿ ln x → 0, (ln x)/xⁿ → 0, log_a(x+1)/x → log_a e,
  log_a x → ∓∞ as x→0⁺ by a, x^a → ±∞ by a — see Errors).
- **Continuity (pp. 20–22):** the three conditions at a
  point (f(a) defined; finite limit; limit = f(a)); a note
  that failing any one condition means discontinuity at a
  (and the graph cannot be drawn without lifting the pen);
  algebra of continuous functions (sum, difference, product,
  quotient, scalar multiple, reciprocal, power); continuity
  of linear, polynomial, rational, nth-root, sin, cos, tan,
  cot functions on their domains; continuity of composites;
  continuity on open and closed intervals (one-sided
  continuity at the endpoints); the intermediate-value
  theorem (f continuous on [a,b], f(a)·f(b) < 0 ⇒ some
  c in (a,b) with f(c) = 0).

#### Lesson 2 — Computation methods (pp. 23–64)

- **§1 By definition, cases 1–9 (pp. 23–31):** ε–δ / M–α /
  ε–N proofs for each of the nine limit types, e.g. Case 2
  (p. 25): lim (7x+2)/(x−1) = +∞ as x→1⁺ and
  lim (x+8)/(1−x) = +∞ as x→1⁻; Case 3 (p. 26):
  lim (x+1)/x = −∞ as x→0⁻ and lim (3x+3)/(2−x) = −∞ as
  x→2⁺. (See Errors for a proof gap in Case 2.)
- **§2 Factorisation (p. 33):** 0/0 forms by cancelling the
  common factor.
- **§3 Irrational forms (pp. 34–44):** rationalising with
  conjugates and with the cube-root conjugate
  (A² + ∛AB + B²); worked examples verified correct, e.g.
  lim (∛x−2)/(x−8) = 1/12 as x→8 (p. 40); lim (∛x−1)/
  (∛(4x+4)−2) = 1 as x→1 (p. 40); lim ∛(x+1)/(√(x²+3)−2)
  = −2/3 as x→−1 (p. 40); lim (∛(1+x²)−1)/x² = 1/3 as
  x→0 (p. 40).
- **§§4–5 Limits at infinity (pp. 45–48):** rational
  functions by leading terms; +∞−∞ forms for irrationals.
- **§6 Trigonometric limits (pp. 49–52):** the standard
  limits applied at x→x₀ and at x→∞, including ∞×0
  forms reduced by substitution, e.g. lim (3x+1)·
  sin(2πx/(x−1)) = 6π, lim (2x+1)·sin(πx/(x+3)) = 6π,
  lim (7x+2)·cos(πx/(2(x+1))) = 7π/2, and a squeeze-theorem
  example lim (1+x)·sin x/(3+x²) = 0 (all p. 52, verified
  correct).
- **§§7–8 Exponential and logarithmic limits (pp. 53–56):**
  formula tables (lim (1+1/x)ˣ = e, lim (1+a/x)ˣ = e^a,
  lim aˣ as x→±∞ by a — all correct) with worked examples.
- **§9 Indeterminate 1^∞, ∞⁰, ∞^∞ (p. 57); §10 0×∞
  (p. 59); §11 limits of real sequences at infinity
  (pp. 60–61).**
- **§12 L'Hôpital's rule (pp. 62–64):** stated and applied
  to 0/0 and ∞/∞ forms, e.g. lim (2x²+x+2)/(x²−1) = 2 as
  x→∞, lim (1+cos πx)/(x−1)² = π²/2 as x→1,
  lim (√(x+7)−3)/(x−2) = 1/6 as x→2, lim (e^{ax}−1)/x = a
  as x→0 (all correct).

#### Lesson 3 — Exercises and solutions (pp. 65–118)

23 numbered worked exercises (លំហាត់គំរ ូទី១–២៣), each with
a full worked solution: proofs by definition (Ex. 1, pp. 65–66:
lim (x²−1)/(x+1) = −3 as x→−2, lim (mx+b) = ma+b,
lim ∛x = 0 as x→0, lim √x = 2 as x→2), then worked
computations across every method of Lesson 2.

#### Lesson 4 — Practice exercises (pp. 119–186)

39 numbered practice sets (លំហាត់អនុវត្តទី១–៣៩), one or two
sets per page, each set a group of limit problems with lined
solution space below. A "national exam practice" block
(លំហាត់ប្រឡងថ្នាក់ជាតិ) begins on p. 167 (set 22). The
exercise statements are typeset text and DO extract from the
text layer (p. 119: 936 chars, p. 130: 1,384 chars, p. 140:
1,570 chars of statement text) — the section is not
image-only. Only the divider pages inside it carry
header/footer text only. Read method for this section:
headings and statements from the text layer, visually sampled
(p. 130 visually verified as a typeset-text exercise page;
p. 167 statements extract as text).

#### Lesson 5 — Solutions to the practice exercises (pp. 187–298)

Full worked solutions to all 39 practice sets (set 1 pp. 187–191,
set 2 p. 191, set 3 pp. 192–195, …, set 39 p. 298). Sampled
solutions read correctly, e.g. set 1: ε–δ proofs with
δ = min(1, ε/3) and δ = min(1, ε/5); lim 8/(x−3) = 2 as
x→7 with δ = min(1, 3ε/2) (p. 188, visually verified — the
text layer flips this fraction, so it reads wrong in text
form only).

### Confirmed book errors and slips (all visually verified)

1. **§1.15, p. 19 — lim x/a at infinity is wrong as printed.**
   The formula box gives lim_{x→±∞} x/a = ∞ (a ≠ 0). As
   x→−∞ the limit is −∞ (for a > 0), and the sign flips if
   a < 0. Correct: the limit is +∞ as x→+∞ and −∞ as
   x→−∞ when a > 0 (reversed when a < 0); a single "∞"
   does not cover both directions.
2. **§1.18, p. 20, item ឌ — the power limit's second branch
   is wrong.** The box gives lim_{x→+∞} x^a = +∞ if a > 1
   and −∞ if 0 < a < 1. Correct: x^a → +∞ as x→+∞ for
   EVERY a > 0 (including 0 < a < 1); the −∞ branch is
   impossible for a power of x. (If the intended function
   were a^x, the 0 < a < 1 value would be 0, not −∞ — so
   the printed −∞ is wrong on either reading.)
3. **Note box, p. 20 — |x| at −∞ is wrong.** The box asks
   for lim |x| and states: as x→+∞, |x| = +∞ (correct);
   as x→−∞, |x| = −∞ (**wrong**). Correct: |x| → +∞ as
   x→−∞ — an absolute value is never negative, let alone
   −∞.
4. **Example 2(a), p. 25 — proof gap (limit itself correct).**
   Proving lim_{x→1⁺} (7x+2)/(x−1) = +∞: the proof derives
   0 < x−1 < 9/(M−7) and asserts α = 9/(M−7) > 0 for all
   M > 0 — but that step needs M > 7 (for M ≤ 7 the
   inequality 9/(x−1) > M−7 is automatic and no such α is
   needed), and the proof then concludes with a different
   α = 9/M, without justification. The α = 9/M stated at
   the end does work (f(x) = 7 + 9/(x−1) > 7 + M > M), so
   the result is right; the derivation as printed has an
   unjustified case split and an unexplained switch of α.
- **Minor imprecisions (p. 21, §2.2):** item 6 says 1/f(x)
  is continuous "where f(x) ≠ 0" — it should be f(a) ≠ 0;
  item 7 says [f(x)]ⁿ is continuous "where n is a real
  number" — that needs n a positive integer (or f > 0) in
  general. And §1.12 item 5 (p. 18) states the composition
  limit without naming the hypothesis that f is continuous
  at L. None of these changes any computed answer.

### Honest negatives — what this booklet does NOT teach

- No derivatives and no integrals (those are the sibling
  Derivatives and Primitives booklets); limits are never
  used here to define a derivative.
- No infinite series (convergence of series is not treated;
  §11 covers only limits of sequences).
- No asymptotes or curve sketching (that is the
  Variation-Graphs booklet); the limit tables are not
  connected to graph behaviour.
- L'Hôpital's rule is stated and applied, never proved; no
  Taylor-series route to limits.
- No numerical or graphical estimation of limits — every
  method is algebraic or definitional.
- No limits of functions of two variables.

### Held-copy defects

- Blank pages (zero text layer on pp. 8, 64, 186, 300;
  header/footer text only — 118 chars — on the pp. 129–185
  dividers; same divider pattern as the
  other booklets): PDF pp. 8, 64, 129, 131, 139, 141, 145,
  147, 157, 159, 161, 163, 165, 185, 186, 300 — the
  pp. 129–185 blanks are divider pages inside the
  practice section, not missing exercises; p. 300 is the
  final blank after the references.
- No duplicated pages, no truncated scan, no unreadable
  pages. The book ends at printed p. 299 (references) + one
  blank page.

### Page mapping

Printed page number = PDF page number throughout (offset 0).

    pp. 175–188.
- Solutions section — pp. 189–236 (Lesson 1 pp. 191–202,
  Lesson 2 pp. 203–212, Lesson 3 pp. 213–222, Lesson 4
  pp. 223–236).
- Appendix: formula tables and key methods — pp. 237–247.
- References — p. 248.

### What the booklet teaches, lesson by lesson

#### Lesson 1 — Primitives and indefinite integrals (pp. 9–30 theory)

- **Primitive (§1, pp. 9–14):** motivated by a falling
  body (v(t) = gt, s(t) = ½gt²); definition F′ = f on a
  domain; verifying primitives by differentiation (F = x³ −
  x² + x for f = 3x² − 2x + 1; F = sin²x − 2 for f = sin 2x;
  F = (x+1)e^(−1/x) for f = (x²+x+1−1/x²)e^(−1/x) — all
  correct); any two primitives differ by a constant
  (proved two ways, including F − G = 1 for a worked pair);
  finding the primitive through a given point/initial
  condition (e.g. f(x) = x² with F(3) = 2 → F(x) = x³/3 − 7;
  f(x) = 1/sin²x with F(π/4) = 1 → F(x) = −cot x + 2).
- **Indefinite integral (§2, p. 15) and basic formulas
  (§3, p. 16):** ∫f dx = F + C; linearity; power, 1/x,
  1/x², √x, eˣ, sin, cos, tan, cot formulas.
- **§§4–6:** exponential and trigonometric integrals,
  composite trig (∫f′(x)·sin f(x) etc.).
- **§7 Substitution (p. 22):** the four-step method and a
  composite-function formula table (∫f′(x)/[f(x)]ⁿ,
  ∫f′(x)/√f(x), ∫f′(x)[f(x)]ⁿ, ∫f′(x)e^{f(x)},
  ∫f′(x)/f(x) = ln|f(x)|, ∫f′(x) sin/cos f(x),
  ∫(1+tan²f(x))f′(x) = tan f(x), ∫(1+cot²f(x))f′(x) =
  −cot f(x) — all correct). Practice 5 (p. 23) with short
  answers, all verified correct (e.g. ∫eˣ(eˣ−1)⁵dx =
  (eˣ−1)⁶/6; ∫(sin x/√x)dx = −2cos√x; ∫2(x−2)cos(x²−4x)dx
  = sin(x²−4x)).
- **§8 Integration by parts (p. 24):** derivation from
  (uv)′; choice rules (polynomial × trig/exp → u =
  polynomial; polynomial × ln → u = ln).
- **§9 Rational functions (pp. 26–27):** partial fractions
  for distinct linear factors, repeated linear factors,
  and irreducible quadratics (Δ < 0); worked example
  ∫(x+1)/((x−1)(x²+1))dx = ln|x−1| − ½ln(x²+1) + C
  (correct). Practice 7 (p. 27) with short answers, all
  spot-checked correct (e.g. ∫dx/(x²+x−2) =
  ⅓ln|(x−1)/(x+2)|; ∫(x²+2)/(x+1)²dx = x − 2ln|x+1| −
  3/(x+1); ∫x⁴/(x²−1)dx = x³/3 + x + ½ln|(x−1)/(x+1)|).
- **§10 Trigonometric techniques (p. 28):** product-to-sum
  identities for sin·cos, cos·cos, sin·sin; sin^m·cos^n
  cases (one exponent even, one odd; both odd; both even
  via half-angle formulas); §10.3 sinⁿx and cosⁿx;
  **§11** rational-fraction techniques.
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 31–41 (spot-checked:
  Q8 F(1) = 4 for f = 2x → x²+3; Q9 F = sin 2x → f =
  2cos 2x; Q11 the non-primitive of 2x is 2x²+1; Q14
  F = ln(x²+1) → f = 2x/(x²+1); Q18 ∫(6x²−4x+3)dx =
  2x³−2x²+3x — all correct); practice exercises pp. 42–64.

#### Lesson 2 — Definite integrals (pp. 65–83 theory)

- **Introduction (§1, p. 65) and definition (§2, p. 66):**
  displacement of a body with v(t) = 3t²+5 from t = 3 to
  t = 5 is 108 m (the constant C cancels); ∫ₐᵇ f dx =
  F(b) − F(a), independent of the constant and of the
  variable name; lower/upper limits, integrand.
- **Fundamental theorem (§3, p. 67):** part 1 (F(x) =
  ∫ₐˣ f(t)dt ⇒ F′ = f) and the Leibniz rule for variable
  limits; worked examples: ∫₁³(3x²−2x+1)dx = 20,
  ∫₀^{π/2} cos x dx = 1, ∫₀¹ e^{2x}dx = (e²−1)/2;
  d/dx ∫₁ˣ √(1+t³)dt = √(1+x³);
  d/dx ∫₀^{x²} sin t dt = 2x·sin(x²) (all correct).
- **Properties (§4, pp. 68–70):** ∫ₐᵃ = 0, reversal flips
  the sign, linearity, Chasles relation, inequalities,
  mean value of a function.
- **§5 Substitution (p. 71):** three steps with change of
  limits; ∫₀² 2x(x²+1)³dx = (5⁴−1⁴)/4 = 156 (correct).
  Practice 1 short answers verified (∫₀¹ x√(1−x²)dx = 1/3;
  ∫₁^e (ln²x)/x dx = 1/3; ∫₀¹ eˣ/(1+eˣ)dx = ln((1+e)/2)).
- **§6 Integration by parts (p. 72):** definite form and
  the **ILATE** priority rule (Inverse trig, Logarithmic,
  Algebraic, Trigonometric, Exponential); worked example
  ∫₁^e x ln x dx = (e²+1)/4 (correct).
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 73–83 (spot-checked:
  Q11 ∫₁³ = 5 ⇒ ∫₃¹ = −5; Q12 ∫₀² = 3, ∫₂⁵ = 4 ⇒ ∫₀⁵ = 7;
  Q14 ∫₀³ f = 6 ⇒ ∫₀³(2f+1) = 15; Q15 ∫₁² f = 2,
  ∫₁² g = −1 ⇒ ∫₁²(3f−4g) = 10 — all correct); practice
  exercises pp. 84–102.

#### Lesson 3 — Plane area (pp. 103–119 theory)

- **Riemann sums (§1, pp. 103–105):** f(x) = x²+1 on
  [0,1] with n = 4 — inscribed (left) sum 1.21875,
  circumscribed (right) sum 1.46875, midpoint sum
  1.328125, exact integral 4/3 ≈ 1.3333, with the
  ordering 1.21875 < 1.328125 < 4/3 < 1.46875 (all correct).
- **Area under a curve (§2, pp. 105–108):** S = ∫ₐᵇ f dx
  for f ≥ 0; S = −∫ₐᵇ f dx for f ≤ 0; sign-changing
  functions split at the zero c, S = ∫|f| dx.
- **Area between two curves (§3)** and **area with respect
  to Oy (§4, p. 109):** S = ∫_c^d [f(y) − g(y)] dy;
  worked example: area between x = y² and x = y+2 is 9/2
  (correct).
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 110–119 (spot-checked:
  Q2 inscribed rectangles use the minimum value; Q7 for
  increasing f, inscribed ≤ midpoint ≤ circumscribed —
  correct); practice exercises pp. 120–144.

#### Lesson 4 — Volumes and arc length (pp. 145–160 theory)

- **Solids of revolution (§1, pp. 145–148):** disk method
  (V = π∫ₐᵇ [f(x)]²dx about Ox; V = π∫_c^d [g(y)]²dy
  about Oy) and washer method (V = π∫ₐᵇ ([f]²−[g]²)dx).
  Worked examples: y = √x revolved about Ox from 0 to 4
  gives V = 8π; the sphere-volume proof (V = 4πR³/3 from
  y = √(R²−x²)); washer example y = x² vs y = 2x on
  [0,2] gives V = 64π/15 (all correct).
- **Arc length (§2, pp. 149–150):** derivation from
  ∆L = √((∆x)²+(∆y)²); L = ∫ₐᵇ√(1+[f′(x)]²)dx and the
  x = g(y) form; worked example y = (4√2/3)x^{3/2} − 1 on
  [0,1] gives L = 13/6 (correct).
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 151–159; practice
  exercises pp. 160–174; chapter exercises pp. 175–188.

#### Solutions (pp. 189–236) and appendix (pp. 237–247)

- Solutions section with answer keys and worked solutions
  for all four lessons (L1 pp. 191–202, L2 pp. 203–212,
  L3 pp. 213–222, L4 pp. 223–236).
- Appendix: formula tables (primitives of elementary and
  composite functions, special primitives including
  ∫dx/cos x = ln|tan(x/2+π/4)|, ∫dx/sin x = ln|tan(x/2)|,
  ∫dx/√(x²±a²) = ln|x+√(x²±a²)|, ∫ln x dx = x ln x − x,
  ∫dx/(x ln x) = ln|ln x|), the initial-value-problem
  method, and two worked initial-value examples
  (f′ = 3x²−4x+5, f(1) = 6 → f(x) = x³−2x²+5x+2; curve
  through A(0,3) with y′ = 2e^{2x}+cos x → y = e^{2x}+
  sin x+2 — both correct).

### Confirmed book errors and slips

- **Cover decoration — De Moivre's formula is wrong as
  printed.** The background art on the cover gives
  (cos θ + i sin θ)² = cos(nθ) + i sin(nθ). Correct:
  (cos θ + i sin θ)² = cos(**2**θ) + i sin(**2**θ) (or
  the left side should carry the power n). Same artwork
  appears on the sibling booklets' covers.
- Otherwise none found: the worked examples, practice short
  answers and MCQ items sampled across all four lessons
  read correctly (the checks above). The MCQ answer keys
  were spot-checked only, not exhaustively re-checked.

### Honest negatives — what this booklet does NOT teach

- No improper integrals (infinite limits or unbounded
  integrands); every definite integral here has finite
  limits and a continuous integrand.
- No numerical integration (trapezoidal rule, Simpson's
  rule) — Riemann sums appear only as the motivation for
  the area integral.
- No surface area of revolution; no centres of mass,
  moments, or Pappus's theorems.
- No differential equations (those are the sibling
  Differential Equations booklet); the initial-value
  problems here are only "find F given F′ and one point".
- No parametric or polar curves — area and arc length are
  only for y = f(x) and x = g(y).
- No integration of inverse trigonometric functions as a
  technique (arcsin/arctan appear only as formula-table
  results).

### Held-copy defects

- Blank pages (zero text layer; same divider pattern as the
  other booklets): PDF pp. 8, 64, 95, 102, 144, 188 — at
  lesson/section boundaries, intentional divider blanks;
  no content is missing.
- No duplicated pages, no truncated scan, no unreadable
  pages. The book ends at printed p. 248 (references).

### Page mapping

Printed page number = PDF page number throughout (offset 0).


## 6. `g12-probability.pdf` — Grade 12 Probability booklet (Khem Putthi)

**File:** `g12-probability.pdf` — 221 PDF pages, 3,255,533 bytes,
SHA-256 `e1f37df7ab54…` (full hash in `grab-results.json`).
**Read:** text-layer read (Khmer extracts legibly; formulae
fragment across lines) plus visual read of the cover, p. 2
imprint, and p. 23 (error verification). Page numbers below are
PRINTED page numbers; printed number = PDF page number
throughout (verified: TOC footer "7" on PDF p. 7; Lesson 1 opens
on printed p. 9 = PDF p. 9).

### Identity — NOT a MoEYS publication (correction to the brief)

- **Title as printed (Khmer):** ប្រូបាប — transliteration:
  *Proubab* — **PROBABILITY — GRADE 12** (cover header:
  គណិតវិទ្យាថ្នាក់ទី១២; series code **KP012**).
- **Author:** compiled by លោកគ្រូ ខេម ពុទ្ធី (Teacher Khem
  Putthi); content checked by លោកគ្រូ ឡាយ ចាន់ហេន (Lay
  Chanheng) — same committee page (p. 2) as the other
  booklets in the series.
- **Edition/year as printed:** cover foot: first printing,
  2026. Imprint p. 2: trial version © 2026 (កំណែសាកល្បង © ២០២៦).
- **ISBN:** none printed.
- **Publisher:** no ministry imprint anywhere (ក្រសួង occurs
  zero times in the text layer). Prepared for Grade 12
  self-study and Baccalaureate / outstanding-student exam
  preparation; printing/distribution via បណ្ណាគារអង្គរ (Angkor
  Bookstore). A private exam-preparation booklet, not a MoEYS
  textbook; it nowhere claims to be a companion to, or a
  chapter of, the Grade 12 textbook. References (p. 221) cite
  the MoEYS Grade 12 textbook (basic and advanced levels)
  and Baccalaureate papers as sources.
- **Front matter:** cover (p. 1), committee/imprint (p. 2),
  dedication (p. 3), preface (p. 4), acknowledgements (p. 5),
  table of symbols (p. 6), contents (p. 7), **p. 8 blank**.

### Contents (from the printed table of contents, p. 7)

- Lesson 1: Combinatorics and probability — p. 9
  - 1 Combinatorics (sets, subsets, 2ⁿ subsets); 2 Union,
    intersection, complement of sets; 3 Sample space and
    events; 4 Sample space; 5 Events (elementary,
    impossible, certain); 6 Compound events (product,
    sum, mutually exclusive, complementary); 7 Counting,
    permutation and combination principles; 8 Probability
    (classical definition); 9 Permutations; 10 Combinations;
    11 Properties of probability; 12 MCQ Lesson 1 (100
    items) — p. 31; practice exercises (44) — pp. 40–84.
- Lesson 2: Conditional probability and Bayes' theorem —
  p. 85
  - 1 Probability of compound events; 2 Conditional
    probability; 3 Dependent and independent events;
    4 Bayes' theorem; MCQ Lesson 2 (100 items) — p. 95;
    practice exercises (16) — pp. 106–122.
- Lesson 3: Random variables and binomial distribution —
  p. 123
  - 1 Definition of a random variable (discrete vs
    continuous); 2 Probability distribution of a discrete
    random variable; 3 Expectation, variance, standard
    deviation; 4 Bernoulli trials and the binomial
    distribution; MCQ Lesson 3 (100 items) — p. 138;
    practice exercises (10) — pp. 148–158.
- Baccalaureate question bank (2014–2026) — pp. 159–170.
- Solutions section — pp. 171–216.
- Appendix: summary tables — pp. 217–220.
- References — p. 221.

### What the booklet teaches, lesson by lesson

#### Lesson 1 — Combinatorics and probability (pp. 9–30 theory)

- **Sets (§§1–2, pp. 9–10):** elements, empty set, subsets,
  n(A), 2ⁿ subsets of an n-element set; union, intersection,
  complement.
- **Random experiments, trials, sample space (§§3–4,
  pp. 10–12):** a random experiment's outcome cannot be
  known in advance; a trial is one specified performance;
  sample space S is the set of all possible outcomes
  (two coin tosses: S = {(H,H),(H,T),(T,H),(T,T)});
  two dice: n(S) = 36.
- **Events (§5, pp. 13–14):** an event is a subset of S;
  elementary (single outcome), impossible (∅), certain (S)
  events, worked with balls and cards (e.g. drawing two
  cards numbered 1–4 without replacement: n(S) = 12).
- **Compound events (§6, pp. 15–16):** product event
  (A ∩ B), sum event (A ∪ B), mutually exclusive events
  (A ∩ B = ∅), complementary events (A ∩ Ā = ∅,
  A ∪ Ā = S); worked with one die (A = even {2,4,6},
  B = multiple of 3 {3,6}, C = ≤ 2 {1,2}: A ∩ B = {6},
  B ∩ C = ∅, so B and C are mutually exclusive).
- **Counting principles (§7, pp. 17–20):** addition and
  multiplication principles (with the factorial
  computations 5! = 120, 12!/5! = 3,991,680,
  15!/(4!·8!) = 1,351,350, 9!/(6!·4!) = 21 — all correct).
- **Probability (§8, pp. 21–22):** classical definition
  P(A) = n(A)/n(S); 0 ≤ P(A) ≤ 1; worked examples
  (choosing a class president from 5 girls and 3 boys:
  P(girl) = 5/8 = 0.625, P(boy) = 3/8 = 0.375; drawing
  cards with replacement: P(first card even) = 1/3,
  P(sum = 5) = 2/9 — correct).
- **Permutations (§9, pp. 22–27):** n! arrangements;
  circular (n−1)!; with repetition n^m; P(n,r) =
  n!/(n−r)!; permutations with repeated items (see
  Errors); worked examples all correct (8 students in a
  row: 8! = 40,320; two named students adjacent:
  7!·2! = 10,080; 10 books with 3 always together:
  8!·3! = 241,920; 4-digit codes from {1,…,9} with
  repetition: 9⁴ = 6,561, with P(all four digits even) =
  256/6,561 and P(all four distinct) = 3,024/6,561 =
  112/243).
- **Combinations (§10, p. 28):** C(n,r) = n!/((n−r)!r!);
  worked example: choosing 8 students from 9 boys and 11
  girls: C(20,8) = 125,970; P(all girls) = 165/125,970 =
  11/8,398; P(5 girls, 3 boys) = 38,808/125,970 =
  6,468/20,995 ≈ 0.308 (correct).
- **Properties (§11, pp. 29–30):** probabilities of
  elementary events sum to 1; P(S) = 1, P(∅) = 0;
  worked example: drawing 4 letters from {a,…,j} (3
  vowels, 7 consonants): P(no vowel) = 35/210 = 1/6,
  P(exactly one vowel) = 105/210 = 1/2, P(exactly two
  vowels) = 63/210 = 3/10, P(at least one vowel) = 5/6
  (correct).
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 31–39; practice
  exercises 44, pp. 40–84. The MCQ answer key (p. 171)
  follows a perfect repeating 4-cycle (គ, ខ, ក, ឃ every
  four questions) — spot-checked Q1–Q22 against the
  questions, all agree with the key, so the cycle is how
  the bank was authored, not a key error; it is noted
  here because a perfectly regular key is unusual.

#### Lesson 2 — Conditional probability and Bayes (pp. 85–94 theory)

- **Compound-event probabilities (§1, p. 85):**
  P(A ∩ B) = P(A)·P(B) for independent events,
  P(A)·P(B/A) for dependent events; P(A ∪ B) =
  P(A)+P(B)−P(A ∩ B) (or P(A)+P(B) when mutually
  exclusive); P(Ā) = 1 − P(A).
- **Conditional probability (§2, p. 86):** P(B/A) =
  P(A ∩ B)/P(A); multiplication rule.
- **Dependent/independent events (§3, p. 86):** the three
  equivalent conditions (P(A ∩ B) = P(A)P(B),
  P(A/B) = P(A), P(B/A) = P(B)).
- **Bayes' theorem (§4, pp. 86–87):** total-probability
  formula P(B) = Σ P(B/Aᵢ)P(Aᵢ) and Bayes' formula.
  Worked examples all correct: three machines producing
  50%/30%/20% with defect rates 3%/4%/4% — P(defective)
  = 0.035, and of 1,000 defective items ≈ 429 from A,
  ≈ 343 from B, ≈ 228 from C (P(A|D) = 3/7,
  P(B|D) = 12/35, P(C|D) = 8/35); oranges from
  Battambang (70%, 5% sour) and Kampong Cham (30%, 9%
  sour) — P(sour) = 0.062, P(Battambang|sour) = 35/62 ≈
  0.5645, P(Kampong Cham|sweet) = 273/938 ≈ 0.291.
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 95–105; practice
  exercises 16, pp. 106–122.

#### Lesson 3 — Random variables and binomial distribution (pp. 123–147 theory)

- **Random variable (§1, pp. 123–125):** a function
  X : S → R; discrete vs continuous; the Grade 12 course
  studies discrete variables only. Examples: number of
  heads in two coin tosses, X(S) = {0,1,2}; number of red
  balls in two draws from 3 red + 2 white, Y(S) = {0,1,2}.
- **Probability distribution (§2, pp. 125–128):**
  pᵢ = P(X = xᵢ), 0 ≤ pᵢ ≤ 1, Σpᵢ = 1; distribution
  tables; worked example: three coin tosses, X = number
  of heads, distribution 1/8, 3/8, 3/8, 1/8.
- **Expectation, variance, standard deviation (§3,
  pp. 129–132):** E(X) = Σxᵢpᵢ; E(c) = c, E(aX+b) =
  aE(X)+b, E(X+Y) = E(X)+E(Y), E(XY) = E(X)E(Y) for
  independent X, Y; fair game (E(X) = 0); V(X) =
  Σ(xᵢ−μ)²pᵢ = E(X²) − [E(X)]²; σ(X) = √V(X);
  V(aX+b) = a²V(X), V(X+Y) = V(X)+V(Y) for independent
  X, Y. Worked examples: table (0,1,2,3) with
  probabilities (0.1,0.4,0.3,0.2) gives E = 1.6,
  E(X²) = 3.4, V = 0.84, σ ≈ 0.9165; a 1,000-riel
  lottery ticket (prizes 50,000 × 1, 10,000 × 2,
  2,000 × 5 out of 100 tickets) has E(X) = −200 riel —
  unfair to the player; one die: E = 3.5, V = 35/12 ≈
  2.92, σ ≈ 1.71 (all correct).
- **Bernoulli trials and binomial distribution (§4,
  pp. 132–136):** Bernoulli trial (success p, failure
  q = 1−p); X ∼ B(n,p), P(X = k) = C(n,k)p^k q^(n−k);
  E(X) = np, V(X) = npq, σ(X) = √(npq). Worked
  examples: 5 fair coin tosses — P(exactly 3 heads) =
  5/16, P(at least 1 head) = 31/32, E = 2.5, V = 1.25;
  a shooter with p = 0.8 over 4 shots — P(4 hits) =
  0.4096, P(at least 3 hits) = 0.8192, E = 3.2, V = 0.64,
  σ = 0.8; from E = 6 and V = 4.2, n = 20 and p = 0.3,
  P(X = 2) ≈ 0.0278 (all correct).
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 138–147; practice
  exercises 10, pp. 148–158 (including a two-dice sum
  distribution with E and V, and a distribution table
  with an unknown constant c to solve).

#### Baccalaureate question bank (pp. 159–170)

- One official Baccalaureate question per year, 2014–2019
  and 2021–2026 — **12 questions** (no 2020 question;
  the 2020 Baccalaureate was not held). Each question is
  a probability problem (balls, dice, cards, student
  committees) with a "solution" heading followed by blank
  writing space; the worked solutions are in the
  solutions section (pp. 209–216). No separate "Part 2"
  selected-extra section is printed in this booklet —
  the bank is the 12 official questions only.

#### Solutions (pp. 171–216) and appendix (pp. 217–220)

- MCQ answer-key tables for Lessons 1–3 (100 answers each, on
  pp. 171, 194 and 203) and detailed solutions of the practice
  exercises (Lesson 1 pp. 172–193, Lesson 2 pp. 195–202,
  Lesson 3 pp. 204–208) and of the Bac bank (pp. 209–216).
- Appendix: summary tables of combinatorics and probability
  formulas (addition/multiplication principles,
  permutations, combinations), and of random variables and
  the binomial distribution.

### Confirmed book errors and slips

1. **§9 item ង, p. 23 — permutations with repeated items:
   wrong denominator (visually verified).** The formula is
   printed as P = (n₁+n₂+…+n_p)! / (n₁! × n₂! × … × n_p)!
   — with a factorial sign after the closing parenthesis
   of the denominator. Correct: P = (n₁+n₂+…+n_p)! /
   (n₁! × n₂! × … × n_p!) — the denominator is the
   PRODUCT of the factorials, not the factorial of the
   product. (The worked examples that use this formula
   elsewhere in the booklet, e.g. "BOOK" and
   "PROBABILITY" in the MCQ, use the correct form.)
2. **Cover decoration — De Moivre's formula is wrong as
   printed.** The background art on the cover gives
   (cos θ + i sin θ)² = cos(nθ) + i sin(nθ). Correct:
   (cos θ + i sin θ)² = cos(**2**θ) + i sin(**2**θ) (or
   the left side should carry the power n). Same artwork
   appears on the sibling booklets' covers.
- **Minor imprecision (p. 30):** probability is described
  as a function "P : S → [0,1]" — the domain should be
  the set of events (subsets of S), not the sample space
  itself. No computed answer is affected.

### Honest negatives — what this booklet does NOT teach

- No continuous random variables (named in §1 as a type,
  but the booklet says the Grade 12 course studies
  discrete variables only; no density functions, no
  normal distribution).
- No Poisson, geometric or hypergeometric distributions —
  the binomial is the only named distribution.
- No covariance or correlation; no conditional expectation.
- No statistical inference (no sampling, estimation,
  hypothesis testing); no combinatorics of derangements
  or inclusion–exclusion beyond the two-event union rule.
- No tree diagrams as a named method (the worked examples
  use tables and formulas).

### What the booklet teaches, lesson by lesson

#### Lesson 1 — Vectors in space (pp. 8–15 theory)

- **§1 Coordinates (p. 8):** in the orthonormal frame
  (O,⃗i,⃗j,⃗k) every point P has a unique triple (x,y,z)
  with OP⃗ = x·⃗i + y·⃗j + z·⃗k; x is the abscissa, y the
  ordinate, z the applicate; ⃗i = (1,0,0), ⃗j = (0,1,0),
  ⃗k = (0,0,1) are unit vectors. (See Errors for a slip in
  the §2 formula box on p. 9.)
- **§2 Properties of coordinates (pp. 9–10):** AB⃗ =
  (x_B−x_A, y_B−y_A, z_B−z_A) (correct form pp. 11–12 and
  appendix; p. 9 misprints the y-component — see Errors);
  equality/inequality of vectors componentwise; vector
  addition, subtraction, scalar multiplication, linear
  combinations; modulus |⃗u| = √(x²+y²+z²); distance
  d(A,B) = |AB⃗|. Worked example 1 (p. 9): plotting
  A(1,2,3), B(2,3,5), C(−3,2,4), D(−3,1,4) in the frame,
  with a TikZ 3D diagram.
- **§3 Direction cosines (pp. 10–11):** α, β, γ are the
  angles OP⃗ makes with (Ox), (Oy), (Oz); cos α = x/|OP⃗|
  etc.; cos²α + cos²β + cos²γ = 1.
- **§§4–6 Scalar product (pp. 11–13):** definition
  ⃗u·⃗v = |⃗u||⃗v|cos θ; AB⃗·AC⃗ = |AB⃗||AC⃗|cos θ;
  commutativity; ⃗u ⊥ ⃗v ⇔ ⃗u·⃗v = 0 (non-zero vectors);
  (m⃗u)·⃗v = m(⃗u·⃗v); Cauchy–Schwarz |⃗u·⃗v| ≤ |⃗u||⃗v|;
  triangle inequality |⃗u+⃗v| ≤ |⃗u|+|⃗v|; in an
  orthonormal frame ⃗i,⃗j,⃗k are unit and pairwise
  orthogonal, ⃗u·⃗v = xx′+yy′+zz′; ⃗u ∥ ⃗v ⇔ ⃗u = k⃗v ⇔
  x/x′ = y/y′ = z/z′; cos θ = (xx′+yy′+zz′)/
  (|⃗u||⃗v|); distributivity over sums. Worked example 2
  (p. 13): ⃗u = ⃗i+⃗j+⃗k, ⃗v = ⃗i−⃗j+√6⃗k: |⃗u| = √3,
  |⃗v| = √8 = 2√2, ⃗u·⃗v = √6, cos θ = 1/2, θ = 60°
  (all correct).
- **§7 Midpoint (pp. 14–15):** midpoint of [AB] is
  ((x_A+x_B)/2, (y_A+y_B)/2, (z_A+z_B)/2). Worked
  example 3: six midpoint computations, all correct, e.g.
  A(2,1,1), B(4,3,1) → I(3,2,1); A(−2,3,5), B(3,−1,1) →
  I(1/2,1,3); A(3,−11,−6), B(−4,0,1) → I(−1/2,−11/2,−5/2).
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 16–24 (the Lesson 3
  MCQ bank, p. 83, was spot-checked Q1–Q11 against its
  key — distances, midpoints, direction vectors, triangle
  classification — all correct); practice exercises 11,
  pp. 25–35 (vector algebra, direction cosines, dot
  products, a regular square pyramid, Thales-circle
  orthogonality proof).

#### Lesson 2 — Product of two vectors in space (pp. 36–39 theory)

- **§1 Cross product (pp. 36–38):** definition by the 3×3
  determinant, ⃗u×⃗v = (u_y v_z − v_y u_z)⃗i −
  (u_x v_z − v_x u_z)⃗j + (u_x v_y − v_x u_y)⃗k;
  ⃗u×⃗v is orthogonal to both ⃗u and ⃗v;
  |⃗u×⃗v| = |⃗u||⃗v|sin θ; ⃗u×⃗v = ⃗0 ⇔ ⃗u ∥ ⃗v;
  A, B, C collinear ⇔ AB⃗×AC⃗ = ⃗0; parallelogram area
  |⃗u×⃗v|, triangle area ½|⃗u×⃗v|. Worked example 1
  (p. 37): ⃗u = (1,−2,1), ⃗v = (3,1,−2): ⃗u×⃗v = (3,5,7),
  ⃗v×⃗u = (−3,−5,−7), ⃗v×⃗v = ⃗0 (correct). Worked
  example 2 (p. 38): ⃗u = (√2,√3,2), ⃗v = (−1,1,0):
  ⃗u×⃗v = (−2,−2,√2+√3), |⃗u×⃗v| = √(13+2√6) (correct).
- **§2 Properties (p. 39):** anticommutativity
  ⃗u×⃗v = −(⃗v×⃗u); ⃗u×⃗0 = ⃗0; distributivity;
  c(⃗u×⃗v) = (c⃗u)×⃗v = ⃗u×(c⃗v); ⃗u×⃗u = ⃗0;
  ⃗u·(⃗v×⃗w) = (⃗u×⃗v)·⃗w.
- **§3 Mixed product (p. 39):** ⃗u·(⃗v×⃗w) as a 3×3
  determinant; = 0 ⇔ the three vectors are coplanar;
  parallelepiped volume V = |⃗u·(⃗v×⃗w)|; tetrahedron
  volume V = |⃗u·(⃗v×⃗w)|/6; for four points A, B, C, D,
  V_ABCD = |AB⃗·(AC⃗×AD⃗)|/6 and the height from B onto
  face ACD is h = 3V_ABCD/S_△ACD.
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 40–48; practice
  exercises 9, pp. 49–57 (unit-vector cross products,
  orthogonality proofs, parallelogram/triangle areas,
  mixed products, parallelepiped volumes).

#### Lesson 3 — Applications of the vector product (pp. 58–82 theory)

- **§1 Distance between two points (p. 58):** the 3D
  distance formula, with a diagram of the |Δx|, |Δy|,
  |Δz| legs.
- **§§2–6 Equations of lines (pp. 58–62):** parametric
  form x = x₀+at, y = y₀+bt, z = z₀+ct (t ∈ R) and
  symmetric form (x−x₀)/a = (y−y₀)/b = (z−z₀)/c
  (a,b,c ≠ 0); line through two points; line as the
  intersection of two planes (direction ⃗u = ⃗n₁×⃗n₂,
  find a point by setting z = t); line through a point
  perpendicular to a plane (the plane's normal is the
  direction vector). Worked examples 1–4, all correct:
  Ex. 1 (pp. 59–60) line through A(1,−2,3) with direction
  (−1,2,−3); Ex. 2 (pp. 60–61) line (AB) through
  A(−2,1,−3), B(3,−2,−1): (x+2)/5 = (y−1)/(−3) =
  (z+3)/2; Ex. 3 (pp. 61–62) intersection of
  (P): x+2y+5z−2 = 0 and (Q): 2x−2y+z−1 = 0:
  direction (12,9,−6) = 3(4,3,−2), point M(1,1/2,0);
  Ex. 4 (p. 63) line through A(4,1,2) perpendicular to
  2x+2y−z+1 = 0: (x−4)/2 = (y−1)/2 = (z−2)/(−1).
- **§7 Equations of planes (pp. 63–74):** plane through
  A(x₀,y₀,z₀) with normal ⃗n = (a,b,c):
  a(x−x₀)+b(y−y₀)+c(z−z₀) = 0, i.e. ax+by+cz+d = 0;
  then the eight cases of §7.1–§7.8, each with a worked
  example, all correct: Ex. 5 (p. 64) plane through
  A(1,2,3), ⃗n = (2,−3,6): 2x−3y+6z−14 = 0; Ex. 6
  (pp. 65–66) plane through (L₁) parallel to (L₂):
  11x−2y+14z−63 = 0; Ex. 7 (p. 66) two skew lines —
  the system gives 21 = 2, so no intersection point,
  and the plane through (L₁) parallel to (L₂) is
  3x+y−5 = 0; Ex. 8 (p. 67) plane through A(−3,1,0)
  perpendicular to a line: −4x+3y+7z−15 = 0; Ex. 9
  (pp. 68–69) plane through two parallel lines:
  6x+2y−3z+10 = 0; Ex. 10 (pp. 69–70) mediator plane of
  [AB], A(1,2,3), B(−3,4,1): 2x−y+z+3 = 0; Ex. 11
  (pp. 70–71) plane (ABC) through A(1,2,1), B(−1,3,2),
  C(5,2,2): x+6y−4z−9 = 0; Ex. 12 (pp. 71–72) tangent
  plane to sphere (x−2)²+(y+1)²+(z−1)² = 9 at
  A(1,−3,3): −x−2y+2z−11 = 0; Ex. 13 (pp. 72–74) plane
  through two intersecting lines: 3x+2y−7 = 0.
  Two short drills (ប្រតិបត្តិ) sit inside the theory:
  drill 1 (p. 79, sphere equations) and drill 2 (p. 81,
  points inside two concentric spheres).
- **§8 Angle between two planes (pp. 74–75):**
  cos θ = |⃗n₁·⃗n₂|/(|⃗n₁||⃗n₂|), 0° ≤ θ ≤ 90°;
  (P) ⊥ (Q) ⇔ ⃗n₁·⃗n₂ = 0; (P) ∥ (Q) ⇔ ⃗n₁ = k⃗n₂.
  Worked example 14: planes x−2y+z = 0 and 2x+3y−2z = 0:
  cos θ = 6/√102 ≈ 0.5941, θ ≈ 53.55°; their
  intersection line is x = t, y = 4t, z = 7t (correct).
- **§9 Distance from a point to a plane (p. 76):**
  d = |ax₀+by₀+cz₀+d|/√(a²+b²+c²). Worked example 15:
  d = 2 in both cases, e.g. P₀(−1,3,5) to
  x−2y+2z+3 = 0 gives |−1−6+10+3|/3 = 2 (correct).
- **§10 Distance from a point to a line (p. 77):**
  D(A,(L)) = |PA⃗×⃗u|/|⃗u|.
- **§11 Equation of a sphere (pp. 77–79):** standard form
  (x−a)²+(y−b)²+(z−c)² = r² and general form
  x²+y²+z²+Ax+By+Cz+D = 0. Worked example 16: centre
  (2,−3,1), r = 4; diameter [AB] gives centre (2,1,2),
  r = √3; sphere tangent to the (xz)-plane with centre
  (1,−2,−1) has r = |y_C| = 2; completing the square on
  x²+y²+z²−2x+4y−6z+10 = 0 gives centre (1,−2,3), r = 2
  (all correct).
- **§§12–13 Interior/exterior regions (pp. 80–81):**
  (x−a)²+(y−b)²+(z−c)² < r² inside, > r² outside.
  Worked example 17: sphere centre (−1,−2,1), r = √13.
- **§14 Sphere–plane position (p. 82):** d > r no cut,
  d = r tangent at H, d < r cuts a circle of radius
  r′ = √(r²−d²).
- **§15 Sphere–sphere position (p. 82):** d > r+r′ separate;
  d = r+r′ externally tangent; |r−r′| < d < r+r′ cut in
  a circle; d = |r−r′| internally tangent; d < |r−r′|
  one inside the other.
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 83–92 (Q100 on p. 92
  is the sphere–sphere case d = |r−r′|); practice
  exercises 75, pp. 93–191 — a long set that includes
  Baccalaureate questions, e.g. p. 191 item 24 is a 2026
  Baccalaureate question (20 points) on coplanarity of
  four points, and a 2D parabola question sits among the
  space-geometry practice.

#### Solutions (pp. 192–256) and appendix (pp. 257–264)

- MCQ answer-key tables for Lessons 1–3 (100 answers each,
  on pp. 192, 198 and 203) and detailed solutions of the
  practice exercises (Lesson 1 pp. 193–197, Lesson 2
  pp. 199–202, Lesson 3 pp. 204–235). Sampled solutions
  read correctly throughout (e.g. L1 Ex. 1: ⃗x = (13,31,−34)
  and (−6,−22,26); L1 Ex. 3 direction cosines of (3,−4,5):
  (3√2/10, −2√2/5, √2/2); L2 Ex. 2 cross products
  (−1,−1,−1), (−2,0,−1), (13,6,−1), (−2,3,−1), (0,0,54),
  (0,42,0); L3 Ex. 1 regular tetrahedron: OA⃗·OB⃗ = −x²/3,
  cos ∠AOB = −1/3, AB = (2√6/3)x).
- Appendix 1 (pp. 257–260): summary formula tables for the
  whole booklet — coordinates, dot product, cross product,
  triple product and volumes, line equations and relative
  positions of two lines, plane equations and relative
  positions of two planes, distances and angles (including
  skew-line distance |(⃗u₁×⃗u₂)·A₁A₂⃗|/|⃗u₁×⃗u₂| and the
  line–plane angle sin α = |⃗u·⃗n|/(|⃗u||⃗n|)), sphere
  equations and relative positions.
- Appendix 2 (p. 261): plane-geometry area/perimeter
  formulas and a special-angle trig table (0°–180°) with
  sum/difference, double-angle, sine-rule and cosine-rule
  formulas.
- Appendix 3 (pp. 262–263): Khmer–English–French glossary
  of 40 technical terms.
- Appendix 4 (p. 264): Greek alphabet and the symbols used
  in space geometry.

### Confirmed book errors and slips (all visually verified)

1. **§2, p. 9 — the AB⃗ coordinate formula is wrong as
   printed.** The box gives AB⃗ = (x_B − x_A, **y_B − x_A**,
   z_B − z_A). Correct: the middle component is
   y_B − **y_A**. The same formula is printed correctly on
   pp. 11–12 and in appendix 1 (p. 257), so this is a
   one-off slip in the §2 box.
2. **§7.2, p. 65 — the plane formula's first term is wrong
   as printed.** The box gives (P) : a(x − **x − 1**) +
   b(y − y₁) + c(z − z₁) = 0. Correct: a(x − **x₁**) — the
   subscript on the first x is lost, so it reads as
   "x − 1". The worked example below it (Ex. 6) uses the
   correct form.
3. **§7.4, p. 68 — γ printed as c.** The box defines
   ⃗n = (α, β, **γ**) and then gives the plane as
   (P) : α(x−x₁) + β(y−y₁) + **c**(z−z₁) = 0. Correct:
   the third coefficient is **γ**, not c. The worked
   example below it uses the correct numeric normal, so no
   answer is affected.
4. **Practice 1 (§11 drill), p. 79, item 2(a) — a missing
   parenthesis.** Printed "x + 2)² + (y−1)² + (z+3)² − 16 = 0".
   Correct: "**(x + 2)**² + (y−1)² + (z+3)² − 16 = 0" —
   the opening parenthesis before x is missing.
5. **Cover decoration — De Moivre's formula is wrong as
   printed.** The background art on the cover gives
   (cos θ + i sin θ)² = cos(nθ) + i sin(nθ). Correct:
   (cos θ + i sin θ)² = cos(**2**θ) + i sin(**2**θ) (or
   the left side should carry the power n). Same artwork
   appears on the sibling booklets' covers.
- **Minor inconsistency (not an error in any answer):**
  worked example 2 (p. 13) defines the angle between two
  vectors as acute (0° ≤ θ ≤ 90°), while appendix 1
  (p. 257) gives the vector-angle cosine with
  0° ≤ θ ≤ 180°. The 60° answer in the example is right
  on either convention.
- **Note on the answer keys:** the Lesson 1 MCQ key (p. 192)
  follows a perfect repeating 4-cycle (ក, ខ, គ, ឃ every
  four questions) — but the questions were NOT authored
  to it: spot-checked Q1–Q11 against the questions, only
  Q10 agrees with the key (Q1 correct គ vs key ក; Q2 ក
  vs ខ; Q3 ខ vs គ; Q4 ក vs ឃ; Q5 ខ vs ក; Q6 គ vs ខ;
  Q7 ក vs គ; Q8 ក vs ឃ; Q9 ខ vs ក; Q11 ក vs គ), and
  Q12–Q20 only Q14 and Q15 agree. The cycle is a key
  defect, not how the bank was authored: the Lesson 1 key
  is unreliable as printed and should not be used without
  re-checking each answer. The Lesson 2
  (p. 198) and Lesson 3 (p. 203) keys are irregular.

### Honest negatives — what this booklet does NOT teach

- No conic sections in space (parabolas etc. appear only as
  one 2D practice question); no cylindrical or spherical
  coordinates.
- No vector functions, curves in space, or tangent/normal
  vectors to curves.
- No quadric surfaces (ellipsoids, hyperboloids, cones,
  cylinders as surfaces).
- No matrices or determinants beyond the 3×3 determinant
  used for cross and triple products; no linear systems
  beyond the 2-plane intersection.
- No proofs of the cross-product or triple-product
  properties — they are stated and used.
- No 3D integration or volume by integration (volumes come
  only from the triple product).
- No angle between a line and a plane worked in the theory
  (the formula appears only in appendix 1); no angle
  between two lines worked in the theory (formula only in
  appendix 1).

### Held-copy defects

- No blank pages, no duplicated pages, no truncated scan, no
  unreadable pages. Every page carries text; the book ends
  at printed p. 265 (references).

### Page mapping

Printed page number = PDF page number throughout (offset 0).


## 8. `g12-variation-graphs.pdf` — Grade 12 Variation and Graphs booklet (Khem Putthi)

**File:** `g12-variation-graphs.pdf` — 733 PDF pages, 5,349,049 bytes,
SHA-256 `f650ac5fe20e…` (full hash in `grab-results.json`).
**Read:** text-layer read plus visual read of the cover, p. 2
imprint, and pp. 9, 26–33, 44, 185–189, 192, 248, 253, 255–256,
259, 415, 520, 571 (error verification and key-table reads).
Page numbers below are PRINTED page numbers; printed number =
PDF page number throughout (verified: TOC footer "7" on PDF p. 7;
Lesson 1 opens on printed p. 9 = PDF p. 9; footer "65" on PDF
p. 65 of the sibling booklet, same series layout).

**Method note (read this before trusting any single fraction):**
the Khmer text layer is legacy-font mojibake in places and it
frequently flips numerator and denominator in inline fractions
(e.g. it prints "(x−1)/(2x+3)" as if it were "(2x+3)/(x−1)").
Several apparent errors in the MCQ banks turned out to be
text-layer artefacts; every suspected error listed below was
confirmed against a 150-dpi render of the printed page before
being recorded, and every question whose key looked wrong was
re-checked the same way.

### Identity — NOT a MoEYS publication (correction to the brief)

- **Title as printed (Khmer):** សិក្សាអថេរភាព និងសង់ក្រាបនៃអនុគមន៍
  — transliteration: *Seuksa Athepheap ning Sang Krab ney
  Anukom* — **STUDY OF FUNCTIONS AND GRAPH SKETCHING —
  GRADE 12** (cover header: គណិតវិទ្យាថ្នាក់ទី១២; series code
  **KP012**). The cover subtitle box is "TRIGONOMETRY ·
  CALCULATION METHODS · EXERCISES AND SOLUTIONS — for
  Baccalaureate and outstanding-student exam preparation"
  (Khmer: ត្រីកោណមាត្រ • វិធីសាស្ត្រគណនា • លំហាត់ និង
  ដំណោះស្រាយ).
- **Author:** compiled by លោកគ្រូ ខេម ពុទ្ធី (Teacher Khem
  Putthi); content checked by លោកគ្រូ ឡាយ ចាន់ហេន (Lay
  Chanheng) — same committee page (p. 2) as the other booklets
  in the series.
- **Edition/year as printed:** cover foot: first printing,
  2026. Imprint p. 2: trial version © 2026 (កំណែសាកល្បង © ២០២៦).
- **ISBN:** none printed.
- **Publisher:** no ministry imprint anywhere. Prepared for Grade 12
  self-study and Baccalaureate / outstanding-student exam
  preparation; printing/distribution via បណ្ណាគារអង្គរ (Angkor
  Bookstore). A private exam-preparation booklet, not a MoEYS
  textbook; it nowhere claims to be a companion to, or a
  chapter of, the Grade 12 textbook. References (p. 733) cite
  the MoEYS Grade 12 textbooks (basic and advanced levels,
  2010–2025), the MoEYS teacher guide, Baccalaureate papers
  2014–2025, outstanding-student exam papers, and Stewart (8th
  ed., 2016), Thomas (14th ed., 2018), Larson (11th ed., 2018)
  and Spivak (4th ed., 2008).
- **Front matter:** cover (p. 1), committee/imprint (p. 2),
  dedication (p. 3, to his wife Ngoy Sokunthea and daughter
  Khem Sophy — different from the sibling booklets),
  preface (p. 4), acknowledgements (p. 5, to MoEYS and the
  committee), table of symbols (p. 6), contents (p. 7),
  p. 8 blank. The preface says the booklet covers the three
  lessons below, is drawn with TikZ graphs, and gives each
  exercise a lined Seyes-grid workspace; Lesson 3 closes with
  a mixed "Chapter 4" exercise bank "for measuring ability".

### Contents (from the printed table of contents, p. 7)

- Lesson 1: Rational functions — p. 9
  - 1 Domain — p. 9; 2 Sign of a quadratic — p. 10;
    3 Direction of variation — p. 11; 4 Relative maximum
    and minimum — p. 12; 5 Relative position — p. 14;
    6 Even and odd functions — p. 14; 7 Symmetry axis and
    centre — p. 15; 8 Tangent to a curve — p. 16;
    9 Asymptotes — p. 16; 10 Study plan and graph
    sketching — p. 21; 11 f(x) = (ax²+bx+c)/(px+q) —
    p. 22; 12 f(x) = (ax²+bx+c)/(px²+qx+r) — p. 23.
    Multiple Choice Questions — 100 items, pp. 25–33;
    practice exercises (84) — pp. 34–177.
- Lesson 2: Exponential functions — p. 179
  - 1 Definition — p. 179; 2 Properties — p. 179;
    3 Equations — p. 179; 4 Derivatives — p. 180;
    5 Limits — p. 182; 6 Study of a function — p. 183.
    Multiple Choice Questions — 100 items, pp. 185–193;
    practice exercises (21) — pp. 194–235.
- Lesson 3: Logarithmic functions — p. 237
  - 1 Definition — p. 237; 2 Properties — p. 237;
    3 Derivatives — p. 238; 4 Limits — p. 242;
    5 Equations and inequalities — p. 242; 6 Study of a
    function — p. 246. Multiple Choice Questions — 100
    items, pp. 252–260; practice exercises 1–10 —
    pp. 261–268; "Chapter 4 exercises" 1–76 —
    pp. 269–413.
- Lesson 4: Solutions — p. 415 (Lesson 1 solutions p. 415,
  Lesson 2 p. 520, Lesson 3 p. 571).
- Homework (លំហាត់កិច្ចការផ្ទះ) — p. 718. Appendix: formula
  tables and methods — p. 723. References — p. 733.

### What the booklet teaches, lesson by lesson

#### Lesson 1 — Rational functions (pp. 9–24 theory)

- **§1 Domain (p. 9):** domain recap for the function types
  used in the book: polynomial f(x) = a_nx^n + … + a_0 has
  domain R; f(x) = e^{U(x)} has domain R wherever U is
  defined; f(x) = ln[g(x)] needs g(x) > 0; f(x) = P(x)/Q(x)
  needs Q(x) ≠ 0; f(x) = √g(x) needs g(x) ≥ 0 (see Errors:
  the √g(x) bullet as printed says "Q(x) ≥ 0"). Worked
  example 1 (p. 9): six domain computations, e.g.
  f(x) = √(x+5), √(2x²−8x+6), √(x²+4x+4), √(7x−14),
  √(16−4x²), √(4−(x+1)²).
- **§2 Sign of a quadratic (p. 10):** sign tables; discriminant
  Δ = b²−4ac and the reduced discriminant Δ′ = b′²−ac with
  b′ = b/2, roots (−b′±√Δ′)/a.
- **§3 Direction of variation (p. 11):** f increases where
  f′ > 0, decreases where f′ < 0.
- **§4 Relative maximum/minimum (p. 12):** first-derivative
  (sign change) and second-derivative tests; inflection
  points where f′′ changes sign.
- **§5 Relative position (p. 14):** curve vs. line from the
  sign of f(x) − y.
- **§6 Even/odd functions (p. 14):** f(−x) = f(x) even,
  f(−x) = −f(x) odd.
- **§7 Symmetry axis and centre (p. 15):** x = a is an axis
  iff f(2a−x) = f(x); I(a,b) is a centre iff
  f(2a−x) + f(x) = 2b; axis/centre found by translation.
- **§8 Tangent (p. 16):** slope m = f′(x₀); tangent equation
  y − y₀ = f′(x₀)(x − x₀).
- **§9 Asymptotes (p. 16):** vertical (limit ±∞), horizontal
  (limit b), oblique y = ax+b with
  lim[f(x) − (ax+b)] = 0.
- **§10 Study plan (p. 21):** the standard order — domain,
  limits, asymptotes, derivative and variation table,
  extrema, intercepts, relative position, sketch.
- **§§11–12 The two rational forms (pp. 22–23):**
  f(x) = (ax²+bx+c)/(px+q), domain x ≠ −q/p; and
  f(x) = (ax²+bx+c)/(px²+qx+r), domain by the
  discriminant of the denominator.
- **Worked examples:** Ex. 2 (pp. 18–19) asymptotes;
  Ex. 3 (p. 22) full study of (x²+3x+3)/(x+2);
  Ex. 4 (p. 23) full study of (x²+x−12)/(2x²−9x+4);
  Ex. 5 (p. 24) full study of (x²−6x+8)/(3x²−18x+25).
  Drills 1–2 (pp. 19–20) on asymptotes.
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 25–33 — every question
  checked against the printed key table on p. 415 (read
  from a render; the text layer scrambles the key grid):
  97 of 100 key entries are correct; the 3 errors are
  Q9, Q60 and Q81 (see Errors). One curiosity: Q41 gives
  f(x) = x²/(x−1) with options ក x(x−2)/(x−1)² and
  ខ (x²−2x)/(x−1)² — the two options are the same
  expression — and option គ "both a and b correct"; the
  key គ is right. Practice exercises 84, pp. 34–177:
  long multi-part Bac-style function studies (e.g. Ex. 46
  p. 100; Ex. 70 p. 148; Ex. 84 p. 176), each statement
  page followed by a blank Seyes-grid workspace page.

#### Lesson 2 — Exponential functions (pp. 179–184 theory)

- **§1 Definition (p. 179):** y = e^x, domain R.
- **§2 Properties (p. 179):** e^{m+n} = e^m·e^n,
  e^{m−n} = e^m/e^n, e^{−n} = 1/e^n,
  (e^m)^n = e^{mn}; e^x > 0 for all x ∈ R.
- **§3 Equations (p. 179):** e^x = a ⇔ x = ln a (a > 0);
  exponential inequalities.
- **§4 Derivatives (p. 180):** (e^x)′ = e^x,
  (e^{u(x)})′ = u′(x)e^{u(x)}. Worked example 1 (p. 180):
  derivatives of 12 exponential functions.
- **§5 Limits (p. 182):** lim e^x = 0 at −∞, +∞ at +∞;
  lim x^n e^x = 0 at −∞; lim e^x/x^n = +∞ at +∞;
  lim (1+x)^{1/x} = e; lim (1+a/x)^x = e^a;
  lim (e^x−1)/x = 1; lim (e^{ax}−1)/(ax) = 1.
  Worked example 2 (p. 182) on limits.
- **§6 Study (p. 183):** full study of an exponential
  function. Worked example 3 (p. 183): study of xe^x;
  worked example 4 (p. 184): study of x²e^{−x}.
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 185–193 — every question
  checked against the printed key table on p. 520 (read
  from a render): the key is ក for 99 of 100 questions
  (only Q2 = ខ, for the domain of e^{1/x}), and all 100
  key entries are mathematically correct as printed (see
  the note on the keys below). Practice exercises 21,
  pp. 194–235. Ex. 1 (p. 194): study of f(x) = e^x−x−1
  (minimum 0 at x = 0; oblique asymptote y = −x−1 as
  x → −∞; tangent parallel to y = x at x = ln 2).
  Last exercise is Ex. 21 (p. 234).

#### Lesson 3 — Logarithmic functions (pp. 237–251 theory)

- **§1 Definition (p. 237):** ln defined for x > 0.
- **§2 Properties (p. 237):** e^x = k ⇒ x = ln k;
  ln(ab) = ln a + ln b; ln(a/b) = ln a − ln b;
  e^{ln k} = k; ln(e^k) = k. Worked example 1: values
  of ln.
- **§3 Derivatives (p. 238):** (ln x)′ = 1/x,
  (ln u)′ = u′/u. Worked examples 2–3: derivatives
  including ln(x²+3x), ln(cos x), ln((x²−4)³),
  ln(x+√(1+x²)), x ln x − x. Drills 1–3 (pp. 240–242):
  logarithmic differentiation; more derivatives; limits
  including lim x ln x as x → 0⁺.
- **§4 Limits (p. 242):** lim ln x = −∞ at 0⁺, +∞ at +∞;
  lim (ln x)/x = 0 at +∞; lim x ln x = 0 at 0⁺.
- **§5 Equations and inequalities (pp. 242–245):**
  ln a = ln b ⇒ a = b. Worked example 4: equations
  including ln(x−2)+ln(x+2) = ln 45 and
  3e^{2x}+e^x−10 = 0; worked example 5: inequalities;
  worked example 6: systems of equations.
- **§6 Study (p. 246):** full study of a logarithmic
  function. Worked example 7 (p. 246): study of
  f(x) = x − ln x; worked example 8 (p. 247): study of
  f(x) = x/ln x; worked example 9 (p. 248): study of
  f(x) = e^x ln x (the text layer misreads this as
  e^{ln x}/x, which would be identically 1 — the printed
  function is e^x ln x, confirmed from a render).
  Drills 4–5 (pp. 249–250): tangent and parameter
  problems.
- **Exercises:** MCQ 100 items, pp. 252–260 — every question
  checked against the printed key table on p. 571 (read
  from a render): the key is ក for all 100 questions, and
  all 100 key entries are mathematically correct as
  printed (see the note on the keys below). Practice
  exercises 1–10, pp. 261–268 (values, graphs, limits,
  equations), then the "Chapter 4 exercises" 1–76,
  pp. 269–413 — a mixed review bank of full function
  studies and Bac-style problems, numbered separately
  from the lesson exercises.

#### Lesson 4 — Solutions (pp. 415–717)

- **Lesson 1 solutions:** MCQ key table p. 415 (varied,
  10×10 grid); detailed practice solutions pp. 416–519
  (exercises 1–84; spot-checked, e.g. Ex. 1 domains:
  R, [−1,∞), (−∞,−1]∪[1,∞), R\{−2}, R\{3}).
- **Lesson 2 solutions:** MCQ key table p. 520; detailed
  practice solutions pp. 521–570 (exercises 1–21, all
  present).
- **Lesson 3 solutions:** MCQ key table p. 571; run-1
  solutions pp. 572–578 (exercises 1–7); additional
  applied exercises 8–10, pp. 579–581 — three word
  problems with worked solutions: Ex. 8 machine
  depreciation V(t) = 250(0.75)^t, V(3) ≈ $105.47;
  Ex. 9 bacteria N(t) = 9e^{0.872t}, N = 738 at
  t ≈ 5.05 days; Ex. 10 population 45,600 → 64,800 in
  10 years, growth rate ≈ 3.58%/yr (annual model) or
  k ≈ 3.51%/yr (continuous model) — all three answers
  re-computed and correct; Chapter 4 solutions
  pp. 581–717 (exercises 1–76). The section ends on
  p. 717 with a retirement/compound-interest problem
  (A ≈ $27,112.64); the continuous-compounding formula
  is stated on p. 716.

#### Homework (pp. 718–721)

- A single long function study, fully worked over four
  pages: f(x) = (x²−8x+11)/(x−3)². Domain R\{3};
  horizontal asymptote y = 1; vertical asymptote x = 3
  (limit −∞); f′(x) = 2(x+1)/(x−3)³; relative maximum
  5/4 at x = −1; x-intercepts A(4−√5, 0), B(4+√5, 0);
  y-intercept C(0, 11/9); three tangents — (T1)
  y ≈ −2.927x+5.163, (T2) y ≈ 0.427x−2.663, (T3)
  y = −(2/27)x+11/9; graph on p. 721. The tangent
  slopes and intercepts were re-computed by hand and
  agree with the printed values.

#### Appendix (pp. 723–732)

- **A — Table of Derivatives (pp. 723–724):** elementary
  and composite functions.
- **B — Primitives & Integrals (pp. 725–726):** basic
  primitives, composite forms, and primitives of special
  rational functions (a > 0).
- **C — Limits and indeterminate forms (pp. 727–728):**
  the 7 indeterminate forms, how to transform them, and
  L'Hôpital's rule.
- **D — The 8 steps of function study and graph sketching
  (pp. 729–730):** parts 1 and 2.
- **E — Tangents & Analytic Geometry (p. 731):** tangent
  and normal equations at M₀(x₀,y₀), and related
  analytic-geometry formulas.
- **F — Trig formulas and special-angle table (p. 732).**

### Confirmed book errors and slips (all visually verified)

1. **§1, p. 9 — the √g(x) domain condition is wrong as
   printed.** The bullet for the irrational function
   f(x) = √g(x) gives the condition "**Q(x)** ≥ 0".
   Correct: "**g(x)** ≥ 0". (The Q(x) belongs to the
   rational-function bullet just above it; the √g(x)
   bullet copied the wrong letter.)
2. **Lesson 1 MCQ key, p. 415, Q60 — wrong key entry.**
   The question asks the vertical asymptotes of
   f(x) = (x+1)/(x²−4). The key says ក, i.e. x = 2 only.
   Correct: គ — x = 2 **and** x = −2 (the denominator is
   zero at both, the numerator at neither).
3. **Lesson 1 MCQ key, p. 415, Q9 — wrong key entry.**
   The question asks the domain of f(x) = 1/√x. The key
   says ខ, i.e. x ≠ 0. Correct: គ — x > 0 (the square
   root needs x ≥ 0 and the fraction needs x ≠ 0).
4. **Lesson 1 MCQ key, p. 415, Q81 — wrong key entry.**
   The question asks the centre of symmetry of
   f(x) = (x+3)/(x−1). The key says ខ, i.e. (−1, 1).
   Correct: ក — (1, 1): the vertical asymptote is x = 1
   and the horizontal asymptote is y = 1.
5. **Cover decoration — De Moivre's formula is wrong as
   printed.** The background art on the cover gives
   (cos θ + i sin θ)² = cos(nθ) + i sin(nθ). Correct:
   (cos θ + i sin θ)² = cos(**2**θ) + i sin(**2**θ) (or
   the left side should carry the power n). Same artwork
   appears on the sibling booklets' covers (see the
   Space Geometry filing).
6. **Lesson 1 banner, p. 9 — decoration slip.** The
   banner artwork over Lesson 1 prints "sinθ + cosθ = 0"
   (twice). That is not an identity; it is background
   decoration, like the cover art.
- **Note on the answer keys (read before using the banks):**
  the Lesson 2 key (p. 520) is ក for 99 of 100 questions
  (only Q2 = ខ) and the Lesson 3 key (p. 571) is ក for all
  100 questions. Every question in both banks was checked
  against its printed key and all 200 key entries are
  mathematically correct — the author simply placed the
  correct answer first in nearly every question. A learner
  who always picks ក scores 99% on Lesson 2 and 100% on
  Lesson 3, so the banks do not test discrimination; this
  is a design weakness, not a key error. (The Lesson 1
  key, p. 415, is varied — 97 of 100 correct, with the
  three errors above.)
- **Note on apparent errors that are NOT errors:** the
  following all looked wrong in the text layer and are
  correct as printed — L1 Q20 (f(x) = (x−1)/((x−2)(x−3)),
  domain x ≠ 2 and x ≠ 3 = គ), Q27 (f(x) = (x−1)/(x+1),
  f′ = 2/(x+1)² = ក), Q71 (f(x) = ln x/(x−1); x = 0 is a
  vertical asymptote, key ខ), Q73 (y = 2x/(x+1);
  asymptotes meet at (−1, 2) = ក), Q98 (f(x) = (x²+4)/x,
  minimum value 4 = ខ); L2 Q17 ((e²)³ = e⁶ = ក), Q31
  ((e^{√x})′ = e^{√x}/(2√x) = ក), Q51
  (lim (e^{2x}−1)/x = 2 = ក), Q52
  (lim (e^{kx}−1)/x = k = ក), Q88/Q89 (y = e^x/(e^x+1);
  horizontal asymptotes y = 1 as x → +∞ and y = 0 as
  x → −∞, both ក); L3 Q7 (f(x) = √(ln x), domain
  [1, +∞) = ក), Q34 ((ln √x)′ = 1/(2x) = ក), Q39
  ((ln(x+√(x²+1)))′ = 1/√(x²+1) = ក), Q55
  (lim ln x/(x²−1) = 1/2 = ក), Q57/Q58 (both limits 0
  = ក), Q87/Q88/Q93 (f(x) = (ln x)/x: f′ = 0 at x = e,
  maximum value 1/e, inflection at x = e^{3/2} — all ក).
  In each case the text layer had flipped a fraction or
  misread an exponent.

### Honest negatives — what this booklet does NOT teach

- No trigonometric-function studies: sin/cos appear only
  as examples inside derivatives and limits, and in the
  trig appendix — no full study of a sine or cosine
  function, and no inverse trigonometric functions.
- No parametric or polar curves; no conic sections.
- No integration inside the lessons: primitives and
  integrals appear only as a formula table in appendix B;
  there are no worked integrals and no area-under-a-curve
  problems.
- No differential equations beyond the e^x / ln x
  equations and inequalities of Lessons 2–3.
- No sequences or series (the limit (1+1/x)^x → e appears
  only as cover decoration and in the §5 limit list);
  no probability or statistics.
- No vector or 3D content — that is the sibling Space
  Geometry booklet.
- No proofs of the derivative or limit rules — they are
  stated and used; L'Hôpital's rule appears only in
  appendix C, not in the lesson theory.

### Held-copy defects

- Blank pages: 8, 178, 236, 414, 722 — intentional
  separators (p. 8 after the contents; pp. 178, 236, 414
  before Lessons 2, 3 and the solutions; p. 722 after the
  homework).
- Many pages in the practice sections are blank Seyes-grid
  workspace pages that follow each exercise statement
  (e.g. p. 44) — intentional, not defects; the grid is
  the space the learner is meant to work in.
- No duplicated pages, no truncated scan, no unreadable
  pages. The book ends at printed p. 733 (references).

### Page mapping

Printed page number = PDF page number throughout (offset 0).


## 9. `g12-bac-exercises.pdf + g12-bac-papers-2002.pdf + g12-bac-papers-2011.pdf` — Grade 12 baccalaureate papers (exam papers — identity only)

**Status: these three files are EXAM PAPERS, not textbooks.** They are
recorded here for identity only — they are NOT read unit-by-unit and
NOT cited as teaching sources (textbook-definition ruling;
`set-verification.md` § "Exam papers — NOT textbooks"). There is no
separate title page in any of them; the cover carries the title and the
spine carries the full title. Khmer was read visually — the text layers
are legacy-font mojibake.

**Shared identity (all three files).** Same series, same compiler:
compiled by លោកគ្រូ ខេម ពុទ្ធី (Teacher Khem Putthi); every content
page is footered "រៀបរៀងដោយ៖ ខេម ពុទ្ធី — Tel : 092 522 866".
Year **2026** (cover corner "២០២៦"; PDF metadata created 11–12 June
2026, Adobe Acrobat Pro DC 20.6.20042). No ministry imprint, no ISBN,
no named publisher — a private exam-preparation compilation, same
series and cover design as the Grade 12 topic booklets. Page mapping
in all three: **printed page number = PDF page number − 3** (cover +
two front-matter pages unnumbered (contents, spilling onto p. 3 in
the first two files; contents plus a blank p. 3 in the third); content
starts PDF p. 4 = printed p. 1; verified at three or more points per file). No file
contains a 2020 paper (the 2020 Baccalaureate was not held); 2014
appears twice in all three (two sessions). Each paper opens with the
MoEYS exam-form header (candidate/room/seat/session/centre fields,
two exam rules, scissors line), then "Paper 2: Mathematics" for its
stream.

### 1. `g12-bac-exercises.pdf` — Adapted Baccalaureate Exercises 2002–2025

- **File:** 147 PDF pages (printed pp. 1–144), 851,761 bytes,
  SHA-256 `87ffd7c499e6548ebfdd10444416ea50c31c7395c67903fa1dd7e02241e192e8`
  (verified against the file on disk; matches `grab-results.json`).
- **Title as printed:** cover "ត្រៀមប្រឡងបាក់ឌុប — ថ្នាក់ទី ១២ —
  វិញ្ញាសា គណិតវិទ្យា" (Preparing for the Baccalaureate — Grade 12 —
  Mathematics papers); spine "លំហាត់ដែលកែឆ្នៃពីលំហាត់បាក់ឌុប ឆ្នាំ
  ២០០២-២០២៥" (Exercises adapted from Baccalaureate exercises,
  2002–2025).
- **What it is:** 24 ADAPTED papers, numbered 1–24 in red, 2002–2025,
  science stream from 2014 (2014 in two sessions; 2002–2013 carry no
  stream tag). Each paper reworks one year's Bac paper into the
  exam-form layout (150 minutes, parts I–VI/VII: limits, complex
  numbers, integrals, probability, differential equations, space
  geometry/conics, function study) with the numbers changed — e.g.
  Paper 1 uses z^2024 and Paper 23 uses i^2026, so the exponents do
  NOT date the papers; the contents list does. **Bare questions only:
  no worked solutions and no answer key anywhere.** Each paper is 1–2
  pages of questions (44 pages of questions in total) followed by
  blank grid working paper for the student to write on.
- **Contents list:** complete — 24 entries, but it spills onto PDF
  p. 3 (entries 23–24: 2024 at printed p. 133, 2025 at printed
  p. 139; verified visually: Paper 23 opens on PDF p. 136, Paper 24
  on PDF p. 142).
- **Held-copy defects:** 5 fully blank pages (PDF pp. 21, 69, 75, 81,
  87 — end-of-section leftovers where a paper ran short; no content
  is missing). The 95 blank grid pages are intentional working space,
  not a defect. No duplicated pages, no truncated pages, no unreadable
  pages; all 24 papers present.

### 2. `g12-bac-papers-2002.pdf` — Baccalaureate Mathematics Papers 2002–2025 (science stream)

- **File:** 227 PDF pages (printed pp. 1–224), 1,519,734 bytes,
